{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"24:Gradient masking attributes ${\\\\leq}$4\\\\% of the 18--23pp gap to gradient dilution; content quality at inference explains the majority, though forward-pass attention remains an unquantified residual.\\n39:Second, all four models (1.5B--13B) are code-specialized; models at 30B+ parameters may have sufficient capacity to overcome diagnostic signal dilution, and general-purpose LLMs may behave differently. The gradient masking ablation is validated on two architectures (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds; DeepSeek-6.7B, 1 seed); broader cross-architecture and cross-scale validation remains future work.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.45}}"}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"14:Regex B5 (99.33\\\\%) exploits the \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker, providing a non-learned baseline; its collapse to 28.49\\\\% under marker removal (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:nomarker}) shows the SFT advantage is not format-driven.\\n33:Regex B5 (marker) & \\\\multicolumn{4}{c}{99.33} \\\\\\\\\\n46:Under \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, all four models achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.33\\\\% across all available seeds (Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}), spanning only 0.50pp across architectures (the tightest of any condition).\\n57:\\\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\\\ model scale. \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} maintains ${\\\\geq}$99.33\\\\% regardless of scale, while \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity (+4.12pp). \\\\texttt{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}\\n117:The \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format includes a \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\\n123:Regex baselines illustrate this decomposition: B5 (\\\\texttt{[No exception data]}$\\\\to$pass, 99.33\\\\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\\\\% without markers, showing it is entirely format-dependent.\\n170:Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this bound: 99.33\\\\% accuracy under 99.8\\\\% masking (${\\\\leq}$0.3\\\\% dilution of the 20.23pp gap), consistent with the Qwen-7B result.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"}

      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"14:Regex B5 (99.33\\\\%) exploits the \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker, providing a non-learned baseline; its collapse to 28.49\\\\% under marker removal (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:nomarker}) shows the SFT advantage is not format-driven.\\n33:Regex B5 (marker) & \\\\multicolumn{4}{c}{99.33} \\\\\\\\\\n46:Under \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, all four models achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.33\\\\% across all available seeds (Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}), spanning only 0.50pp across architectures (the tightest of any condition).\\n57:\\\\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\\\\ model scale. \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} maintains ${\\\\geq}$99.33\\\\% regardless of scale, while \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} shows monotonic sensitivity (+4.12pp). \\\\texttt{nomarker} (markers removed) shows no scale degradation.}\\n117:The \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} format includes a \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\\\\% accuracy; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).\\n123:Regex baselines illustrate this decomposition: B5 (\\\\texttt{[No exception data]}$\\\\to$pass, 99.33\\\\% with markers) collapses to 28.49\\\\% without markers, showing it is entirely format-dependent.\\n170:Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this bound: 99.33\\\\% accuracy under 99.8\\\\% masking (${\\\\leq}$0.3\\\\% dilution of the 20.23pp gap), consistent with the Qwen-7B result.\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.44}}"

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\\section{Experiments}\\n\\nWe evaluate across two research questions:\\n(1)~Why do complete traces degrade SFT verdict accuracy despite containing all diagnostic information?\\n(2)~Which trace components carry the verdict signal, and how does their density affect learning?\\nAll experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\\\\% CIs.\\\\footnote{Problem-level partition (472 train / 119 test, zero crossover; \\\\S\\\\ref{sec:dataset}). \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} duplicates (48.99\\\\% test; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:dedup_stats}) arise from within-problem variants; deduplication yields negligible ($<$0.1pp) delta.}\\nPairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}.0001$ unless noted.\\nUnder problem-level clustered bootstrap ($n_\\\\text{eff}{=}119$), all cross-tier gaps remain significant ($p{<}.001$); 3 of 80 within-tier pairwise comparisons lose significance after Holm--Bonferroni correction.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}\\n\\\\label{sec:cross_model}\\n\\nTable~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\\\\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families under key conditions; single-seed entries are marked with $^\\\\dag$.\\nRegex B5 (99.33\\\\%) exploits the \\\\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker, providing a non-learned baseline; its collapse to 28.49\\\\% under marker removal (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:nomarker}) shows the SFT advantage is not format-driven.\\n\\n\\\\begin{table}[t]\\n\\\\centering\\n\\\\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\\\\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\\\\pm$std over 2--3 seeds; $^\\\\dag$ = single seed (seed 42). Bold: best per model. head-2048 proxy reported.\\nPer-seed breakdowns for all conditions in Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:full_results}.\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}\\nUnder \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}, all four models achieve ${\\\\geq}$99.33\\\\% across all available seeds (Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}), spanning only 0.50pp across architectures (the tightest of any condition).\\nCodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\\\\%, indicating the 2 persistent errors (\\\\texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:irreducible}) are convergence artifacts; 99.83\\\\% is a floor, not a ceiling.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Scale effects concentrate in Tier~2.}\\nTier~1 signals are scale-invariant across 1.5B--13B (8.7$\\\\times$): both \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} and \\\\texttt{ae\\\\_only} span ${\\\\leq}$1.2pp.\\n\\\\texttt{loop\\\\_only} spans 8.57pp, with CodeLlama-13B below Qwen-1.5B, suggesting architecture matte\\n\\n... [截断: 输出过长，省略 11535 字符] ...\\n\\n full-trace degradation.\\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}\\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\\\\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\\\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).\\nAll four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\\\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.\\n\\nA cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a sharp performance jump localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.\\nAt tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\\\\%, only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\\\\to$512 (+13.2--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\\\\sim}$94--98\\\\%).\\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\\\sim}$400--500 tokens, crossing the globals module dump threshold (${\\\\sim}$384 tokens) and exposing structured execution markers (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).\\nCodeLlama tail conditions use label-only loss (\\\\S\\\\ref{sec:density}), which may inflate its tail accuracies; cross-model tail comparisons should account for this training difference.\\nBeyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\\\\times$seed combinations shows all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\\\%, 98.24\\\\%] (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\\n\\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} captures 95--98\\\\% of \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\\nThe residual 2--5pp gap indicates that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Calibration.}\\n\\\\label{sec:calibration}\\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\\\ 0.171 for \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}, a 63$\\\\times$ gap (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:calibration}).\\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (Table~\\\\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown shows all standard exception types achieve 100\\\\% accuracy, with only \\\\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \\\\texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Length control.}\\nPadding \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\\\\%), indicating the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}\\n\\\\label{sec:debugbench}\\nOn DebugBench \\\\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} 100\\\\%, VOTA 99.77\\\\%, \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} 96.71\\\\%.\\nHowever, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis preserves the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.08}}"}

at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\\\\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.\\n\\\\texttt{tail\\\\_2048} captures 95--98\\\\% of \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.\\nThe residual 2--5pp gap indicates that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Calibration.}\\n\\\\label{sec:calibration}\\nCalibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\\\\ 0.171 for \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}, a 63$\\\\times$ gap (Figure~\\\\ref{fig:calibration}).\\nThis calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.005 under \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} (Table~\\\\ref{tab:calibration_model_level} in Appendix), indicating well-calibrated predictions rather than overfit confidence.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Error analysis.}\\n\\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000; Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:error_overlap}); per-type breakdown shows all standard exception types achieve 100\\\\% accuracy, with only \\\\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a benign \\\\texttt{BaseException} subclass) causing failures (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:exception_type}).\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Length control.}\\nPadding \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} to 2{,}048 tokens, even in a semantically loaded position (after the verdict region), yields identical accuracy (99.83\\\\%), indicating the 18--23pp gap arises from spurious patterns in verbose trace content, not sequence length.\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Supplementary OOD analysis.}\\n\\\\label{sec:debugbench}\\nOn DebugBench \\\\citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the condition ranking is preserved: \\\\texttt{exception\\\\_only} 100\\\\%, VOTA 99.77\\\\%, \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace} 96.71\\\\%.\\nHowever, narrow margins (3.29pp headroom from \\\\texttt{no\\\\_trace}) limit discriminative power; this supplementary analysis preserves the ranking but cannot establish effect sizes comparable to the primary dataset (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.08}}"

3-seed mean 81.44\\\\%$\\\\pm$1.11 (Table~\\\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\\n$^\\\\dag$vs.\\\\ \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} (81.44\\\\%$\\\\pm$1.11); not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp, $n_\\\\text{eff}{=}119$).\\n\\\\end{table}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Complementary evidence.}\\nLabel-only loss on full traces (+4.50pp, 3 seeds) yields a confounded upper bound of ${\\\\leq}$26\\\\% gradient contribution, consistent with the masking estimate (${\\\\leq}$4\\\\%) but not significant under clustered bootstrap (95\\\\% CI [$-$0.6, +9.8]pp).\\nQwen-1.5B shows a similar label-only pattern (17\\\\%/83\\\\%, 3 seeds); CodeLlama and DeepSeek exhibit label-only collapse to majority baseline (28.5\\\\%), limiting this complementary analysis to the Qwen family.\\nThe architecture dependence suggests label-only training is sensitive to model capacity or optimization landscape; the gradient masking ablation provides a more robust, architecture-independent decomposition.\\nCross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B confirms this bound: 99.33\\\\% accuracy under 99.8\\\\% masking (${\\\\leq}$0.3\\\\% dilution of the 20.23pp gap), consistent with the Qwen-7B result.\\nError analysis supports the content quality interpretation: all 217 \\\\texttt{full\\\\_trace} errors occur where tokenizer truncation removes diagnostic markers, manifesting through mechanical truncation rather than semantic confusion.\\n\\n\\\\subsection{Complementary Analyses}\\n\\\\label{sec:additional}\\n\\n\\\\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}\\n\\\\label{sec:trace_length}\\nVarying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\\\\%--81.8\\\\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\\\\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\\\\%, $p{=}.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\\\\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).\\nCritically, removing the token budget entirely (no truncation) yields 81.76\\\\%, identical to head-2048, ruling out truncation as the explanation for the full-trace degradation.\\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\\\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\\\\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 0.5}}"}

- §Mean ± std over 3 seeds (42, 123, 456)
- ‡‡Matched-seed baseline; 3-seed mean 81.44%±1.11 (Table 1)
- †vs. full_trace (81.44%±1.11); not significant under clustered bootstrap (95% CI [−0.6, +9.8]pp, n_eff=119)

Let me verify these numbers against the registry data. For gradient masking on Qwen-7B, I'm seeing seed 42 has no recorded metrics, while seeds 123 and 456 both show 99.33% accuracy, which aligns with the reported mean and standard deviation in the table. The standard deviation calculation checks out at 0.20. Now I'm verifying the other metrics: the 0.61 percentage point delta matches, the full_trace value of 81.76% corresponds to the seed42 registry entry, and the 18.07pp gap between the two is correct. For the combined full_trace plus label-only loss result, I'm cross-referencing the registry values but noticing a discrepancy in what should be the mean. I need to verify which experiments the paper actually uses—there's a distinction between the label_only versions and the qwen7b_full_trace_label_only versions. Using the correct seeds (86.22, 86.89, 84.70) gives me the mean of 85.94±1.12, which checks out. The improvement is 4.50 percentage points, but I'm trying to reconcile whether the gradient contribution calculation of 24.9% aligns with the paper's claim of ≤26%. I'm trying different denominators to match the 26% bound—using the 3-seed full_trace mean (81.44) as baseline gives 4.50/18.39 = 24.5%, while using the matched-seed full_trace (81.76) yields 4.18/18.07 = 23.1%, and checking if the maximum improvement across seeds gets closer to 26%. So the 24.9% calculation checks out — that's 4.50pp divided by 18.07pp, which rounds to the ≤26% bound they claim. Now looking at the cross-architecture validation, the DeepSeek-Coder results show 99.33% accuracy under 99.8% masking, and when I compare the masked versus exception-only performance, there's essentially no dilution of the gap, which aligns with what they found on Qwen-7B.

But I'm catching an inconsistency in how the gap is calculated. The paper reports 20.23pp for DeepSeek, but if I use the matched seed42 baseline (99.33% exception-only versus 79.16% full-trace), that gives 20.17pp. The discrepancy comes from whether they're using the single-seed result or averaging across multiple seeds for the exception-only baseline — and it looks like they're using the multi-seed mean of 99.39%, which produces the 20.23pp figure they cite.

What's interesting is that this choice isn't consistent across models. For Qwen-7B masking, they use the matched seed42 values (99.83% and 81.76%), but for DeepSeek they switch to the multi-seed mean for the upper bound. It's a methodological detail that makes the gap slightly larger and the dilution bound slightly smaller, which is conservative, but the inconsistency is worth noting.

Now I'm ready to compile the full audit report with all the verified findings. I'm verifying the full_trace standard deviation of ±1.11 across all four locations in the paper, and confirming the nomarker results at 97.65% ± 0.96 with per-seed breakdowns. I've spotted a methodological inconsistency where Qwen-7B uses matched-seed gaps while DeepSeek uses multi-seed-mean gaps for calculating the gradient masking improvement, and there are some missing registry metrics for certain seed42 experiments.

Now I'm checking the ECE < 0.005 claim for exception_only across all four models—the registry shows DeepSeek at 0.0064, which exceeds the stated threshold, so I need to reread the paper to see if there's a qualifier I'm missing about which models this applies to. I'm noticing a discrepancy between the paper's claim that ECE values are less than 0.005 and the actual calibration results showing DeepSeek at 0.0064. Even accounting for different binning strategies in ECE calculations, the appendix table should match whatever's stated in the main text, so this is definitely worth flagging as a potential factual error.

Beyond that, I've verified most of the numbers in Tables 1-3 against the registry and they check out, though there are some gaps in the seed42 experiment metrics and an inconsistency in how the gap denominator was calculated between the Qwen-7B and DeepSeek masking analyses.
